4.2.
Enumeration of
\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)

Since minimal SSYT are in bijection with excited diagrams, we immediately obtain a determinant formula to count the former.

Corollary 4.2
.

For a skew shape
\lambda/\mu
we have that

|\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)|\,=\,\det\left[\binom{\vartheta_{i}+\mu_{i}-i+j-1}{\vartheta_{i}-1}\right]_{i,j=1}^{\ell(\mu)},

where
\vartheta_{i}
is the row where the diagonal through the cell
(i,\mu_{i})
intersects the boundary of
[\lambda]
.

Proof.

The result follows by combining Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
2.11
.
∎

For certain shapes, the characterization of minimal SSYT of skew shape allows to directly enumerate them and recover nice known formulas.

The following formulas follow directly from the equivalence with excited diagrams, together with the fact that the complements of
\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)
are non-intersecting lattice paths. Here we give a direct interpretations from the SSYT point of view.

Let
C_{n}=\frac{1}{n+1}\binom{2n}{n}
be the
n
th Catalan number.

Proposition 4.3

(
[
15
, Cor. 8.1]
)
.

For the zigzag shape
\lambda/\mu=\delta_{n+2}/\delta_{n}
, we have that

|\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\delta_{n+2}/\delta_{n})|=C_{n}.

Proof.

The zigzag shape
\delta_{n+2}/\delta_{n}
has only one Lascoux–Pragacz path. By the characterization of minimal SSYT in Theorem
3.9
, every
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\delta_{n+2}/\delta_{n})
is determined by the elements of the inner corners. Also, note that the inner corner of the
n
th column is fixed as
0
because it is always on the first row of the shape. Reading the rest of the inner corners from right to left, we have a word
w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{n-1}
that determines
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\delta_{n+2}/\delta_{n})
.

Each inner corner
w_{i}
is on row
i+1
. Then by condition (i) from Theorem
3.9
we have
w_{i}\leq i
and by condition (ii), the element below
w_{i}
is always
w_{i}+1
. Thus,
w_{i+1}
is always to the left of such element, therefore
w_{i+1}\leq w_{i}+1
. Words
w_{1}w_{2}\cdots w_{n-1}
satisfying
w_{i}\leq i
and
w_{i+1}\leq w_{i}+1
are one of the objects counted by the Catalan numbers
[
28
, Exercise 6.19 u]
.
∎

Proposition 4.4

(
[
16
, Ex. 3.2]
)
.

Let
m
and
n
be nonnegative integers. For the reverse hook
\lambda/\mu=(m+1)^{n+1}/m^{n}
we have that

|\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)|=\binom{n+m}{n}.

Proof.

The reverse hook shape has only on Lascoux–Pragacz path. By the characterization of minimal SSYT in Theorem
3.9
, in every
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}((m+1)^{n+1}/m^{n})
the entries of column
m+1
are fixed to be
0,1,\ldots,n
. Reading the remaining elements on the
(n+1)
th row from left to right, gives the sequence
a_{1},a_{2}\dots,a_{m}
that determine
T
. By Condition (ii) in the characterization of minimal SSYT, we have
0\leq a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq\dots\leq a_{m}\leq n
. The number of such sequences is given by
\binom{n+m}{n}
, as desired.
∎

5.
New relations between the Naruse and the Okounkov–Olshanski formula

In this section we use the explicit non-recursive description of the minimal tableaux in
\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)
of Theorem
3.9
to relate the number
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)
of terms of (
NHLF
) and the number
\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
of terms of (
OOF
).

5.1.
Inequality between the number of terms

Theorem 5.1
.

For a skew shape
\lambda/\mu
we have that
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)\leq\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
.

The result follows immediately from the following lemma that states that after adding one to each entry in a minimal skew SSYT of shape
\lambda/\mu
, we obtain a flagged tableaux in
\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)
. Let
{\bf 1}_{\lambda/\mu}
denote the tableaux of shape
\lambda/\mu
with all
1
s.

Lemma 5.2
.

For a skew shape
\lambda/\mu
we have that

\{T+{\bf 1}_{\lambda/\mu}\mid T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)\}\subseteq\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu),

where
T+{\bf 1}_{\lambda/\mu}
denotes entrywise addition.

Proof.

Let
T
be a tableau in
\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)
. By Condition (i) in Theorem
3.9
, we have that for
(i,j)
in
\theta_{r}
,
T(i,j)\leq ht_{\theta_{r}}(i)
. By Definition
2.2
we have that
ht_{\theta_{r}}(i)\leq i-1
. Thus
T+{\bf 1}_{\lambda/\mu}
is a flagged tableau in
\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)
.
∎

Example 5.3
.

Adding
{\bf 1}_{\lambda/\mu}
to the minimal SSYT in Figure
4(a)

yields a flagged tableau in
\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)
, which in fact is the one depicted in Figure
4(b)
.

Proof of From Theorem
5.1
.

By Theorem
1.1
, we have
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)=|\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)|
. By Lemma
5.2
, we have
|\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)|\leq|\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)|
, and the result follows since by Proposition
2.19
, we have that
|\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)|=\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

As illustrated in Figure
4
, if an excited diagram
D
and a reverse excited diagram
S
have equal (up to adding
{\bf 1}_{\lambda/\mu}
) minimal SSYT and skew flagged tableau of shape
\lambda/\mu
, their corresponding flagged tableau and Okounkov–Olshanski tableau of shape
\mu
may differ.

The next result characterizes the skew shapes where the Naruse hook-length formula and the Okounkov–Olshanski formula have the same number of terms.

\comparenumberterms

Proof.

The inequality
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)\leq\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
follows from Theorem
5.1
. By Lemma
5.2
we have that equality occurs if and only if all the flagged tableaux in
\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)
are minimal. First, define the “maximal” tableau
T\in\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)
as
T(i,j)=i-1
for
(i,j)\in[\lambda/\mu]
, and let
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}
be the Lascoux–Pragacz decomposition of
\lambda/\mu
.

Suppose that there is a border strip
\theta_{k}
which does not reach the top row of
[\lambda/\mu]
. By the height condition in the definition of minimal tableaux (Condition (i) in Theorem
3.9
), it follows that for an entry at
(i,j)
in
\theta_{k}
we have
i-1=T(i,j)\leq ht_{\theta_{k}}(i)<i-1
, a contradiction. Thus all border strips reach the top row of
[\lambda/\mu]
.

Next, suppose that there is a border strip
\theta_{k}
, which has a column
c
of height at least 2 for some
c\leq\mu_{1}
, and let
t
be the topmost row in that column of
\theta_{k}
. We then construct another tableaux
T^{\prime}
from
T
by setting
T^{\prime}(i,j)=T(i,j)-1
for pairs
(i,j)
such that
i\leq t
and
j\leq c
, and set
T^{\prime}(i,j)=T(i,j)
otherwise. Note that since
j\leq c\leq\mu_{1}
we have that
i\geq 2
and thus
T^{\prime}(i,j)=T(i,j)-1=i-2
is well defined. Note also that
T^{\prime}
is still a SSYT in
\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)
. Finally, note that
T^{\prime}
violates the condition on consecutive elements in a column of a border strip for the minimal tableaux since
T^{\prime}(t+1,c)=t>t-1=T^{\prime}(t,c)+1
and
(t+1,c),(t,c)\in\theta_{k}
. Thus
\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)\subsetneq\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)
and we do not have equality between
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)
and
\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
.

Hence we see that the border strips
\theta_{i}
are entirely horizontal in the first
\mu_{1}
columns of
\lambda/\mu
and we have
d-r
of them starting in the first
\mu_{1}
columns. Thus
\lambda_{d}\geq\mu_{1}
. Also, since these border strips reach the top row, they must all cross the diagonal starting at
(r,\mu_{r})
and thus this diagonal extends to the last row of the shape, so
\lambda_{d}\geq\mu_{r}+d-r
, which proves the forward direction. See Figure
10
.

Now, if
\lambda_{d}\geq\mu_{r}+d-r
, then it is easy to see that all border strips
\theta_{i}
reach the top row and they are horizontal in the first
\mu_{1}
columns. The tableaux in
\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)
consisting of the columns past
\mu_{1}
are of straight shape, and since the entries in row
i
are bounded by
i-1
, the strict increase in the full columns implies that such entries have to be exactly
i-1
. For the first
\mu_{1}
columns, since all border strips are horizontal, there are no further restrictions coming from the minimal tableaux. Thus
\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)=\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)
, as desired.
∎

Motivated by this result, we call the connected skew shapes
\lambda/\mu
where we have equality
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)=\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)

slim skew shapes
, see
[
18
, §11]
and
[
17
, §2.1]
1
1

1

The convention for slim shapes in the articles
[
18
, §11]
and
[
17
, §2.1]
matches our condition in the case of
r=1
.
. Next, we show that for slim skew shapes
\lambda/\mu
where there is equality
\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)=\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
, this number is given by a product.

Corollary 5.5
.

For a connected skew shape
\lambda/\mu
satisfying
\lambda_{d}\geq\mu_{r}+d-r
where
d=\ell(\lambda)
and
r=\max\{i\mid\mu_{1}=\mu_{i}\}
, we have that

\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)=\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)=\prod_{u\in[\mu]}\frac{d+c(u)}{h(u)}.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.1
, for such skew shapes
\lambda/\mu
we have the equality
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)=\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
. By definition of
r
we have that
\mu_{1}=\mu_{r}
, and thus
\lambda_{d}\geq\mu_{1}+d-r\geq\mu_{1}
. By
[
19
, Proposition 7.14]
, since
\mu_{1}\leq\lambda_{d}
then
\mathcal{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)=\mathrm{SSYT}(\mu,d)
and so
\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)=s_{\mu}(1^{d})
, which is given by the
hook-content formula
(see
[
28
, Cor. 7.21.4]
and
[
19
, Cor. 7.15]
).
∎

Figure 10
.
Illustration of skew shapes
\lambda/\mu
and their Lascoux–Pragacz decomposition with
\lambda_{d}\geq\mu_{r}+d-r
where
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)=\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
.

5.2.
Comparing terms of hook formulas for skew shapes

A natural question to ask is whether for slim shapes
\lambda/\mu
, the formulas (
NHLF
) and (
OOF
) not only have the same number of terms but they are also term-by-term equal. As the next example shows, the terms can be different.

Example 5.6
.

The shape
\lambda/\mu=322/11
is slim with three excited and three reverse excited diagrams, respectively. See Figure
11(a)
,
11(b)
. The formulas (
NHLF
) and (
OOF
), respectively give

\displaystyle f^{\lambda/\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{5!}{5\cdot 4\cdot 3\cdot 2^{2}\cdot 1}\left(5\cdot 3+5\cdot 1+2\cdot 1\right),

\displaystyle=\frac{5!}{5\cdot 4\cdot 3\cdot 2^{2}\cdot 1}\left(4\cdot 2+4\cdot 2+3\cdot 2\right)=11.

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 11
.
For the shape slim shape
\lambda/\mu=322/11
, an illustration of its (a) Excited diagrams, (b) reverse excited diagrams, and (c) NE excited diagrams.

However, there is a reformulation of (
NHLF
) from
[
17
, §3.4]
that is then term-by-term equal to (
OOF
) for slim shapes. To see this, we need the following variation of excited diagrams from
[
17
, §3.4]
. Given a slim skew shape
\lambda/\mu
with
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{\ell})
, we denote by
\widehat{\mu}=(0^{d-\ell},\mu_{\ell},\mu_{\ell-1},\ldots,\mu_{1})
the horizontal flip of
\mu
. Since
\lambda/\mu
is slim then the Young diagram
[\widehat{\mu}]\subset[\lambda/\mu]
. A
NE-excited diagram
is a subdiagram of
[\lambda]
obtained from the diagram
[\widehat{\mu}]
after a sequence of moves
D\mapsto D^{\prime}
where
(i,j)
is replaced by
(i-1,j+1)
if
(i,j)
is in the diagram
D
and all of
(i-1,j),(i-1,j+1),(i,j+1)
are in
[\lambda]\setminus D
. See Figure
11(c)
for an example. The set of these diagrams is denoted by
\mathcal{E}^{\nearrow}(\lambda/\mu)
. From
[
17
, §3.4]
, flipping horizontally the diagrams gives a bijection between
\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)
and
\mathcal{E}^{\nearrow}(\lambda/\mu)
. Moreover, as a consequence of the multivariate identity in
[
17
, Thm. 3.12]
there is a Naruse-type formula for
f^{\lambda/\mu}
in terms of the diagrams in
\mathcal{E}^{\nearrow}(\lambda/\mu)
that appears in
[
23
, §9.1]
.

Corollary 5.7

(
[
17
, §3.4]
,
[
23
, §9.1]
)
.

For a slim skew shape
\lambda/\mu
of size
n
we have that

(5.1)

f^{\lambda/\mu}\,=\,\frac{n!}{\prod_{u\in[\lambda]}h(u)}\sum_{D\in\mathcal{E}^{\nearrow}(\lambda/\mu)}\prod_{(i,j)\in D}h(i,j).

Next, we show that for slim shapes
\lambda/\mu
, there is bijection between the reverse excited diagrams and the NE-excited diagrams of
\lambda/\mu
that preserve the weights of (
OOF
) and (
5.1
), respectively.

Definition 5.8
.

Given an excited diagram
D
in
\mathcal{E}^{\nearrow}(\lambda/\mu)
, let
T:=\Psi(D)
be the tableau
\mu
where
T(x,y)=d+1-i_{x}
where
(i_{x},j_{y})
is the cell in
D
corresponding to
(d+1-x,y)
in
[\widehat{\mu}]
. That is
i_{x}
is the row where the cell
(d+1-x,y)\in[\widehat{\mu}]
ended up in after the excited moves. See Figure
12
for an example.

Proposition 5.9
.

For a slim shape
\lambda/\mu
, the map
\Psi
defined above is a weight preserving bijection between
\mathcal{E}^{\nearrow}(\lambda/\mu)
and
\mathcal{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
. That is, if
\Psi(D)=T
, then

\prod_{(i,j)\in D}h(i,j)=\prod_{(i,j)\in[\mu]}(\lambda_{d+1-T(i,j)}+i-j).

Proof.

We need to prove that
\Psi
is a well-defined map between
\mathcal{E}^{\nearrow}(\lambda/\mu)
and
\mathcal{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
. For
D
in
\mathcal{E}^{\nearrow}(\lambda/\mu)
, the tableau
T:=\Psi(D)
of shape
\mu
is semistandard by a similar argument as the one in the proof of
[
16
, Prop. 3.6]
(induction on the number of excited moves). Also by construction, the entries in
T
are bounded by
d
and so
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\mu,d)
. Since
\lambda/\mu
is slim, by the argument in the proof of Corollary
5.5
we have that
\mathcal{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)=\mathrm{SSYT}(\mu,d)
. Thus
\Psi
is well defined.

Next, we show that
\Psi
is bijective by building its inverse. Given
T
in
\mathcal{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
, let
D:=\Omega(T)
be the set
\{(d+1-T(x,y),y-x+T(x,y))\mid(x,y)\in[\mu]\}
. This map is well-defined by an argument similar to the one in the proof of
[
16
, Prop. 3.6]
. By construction, we have that
\Omega=\Psi^{-1}
, as desired.

Finally, we show that
\Psi
is weight preserving. If
\Psi(D)=T
with
T(x,y)=d+1-i_{x}
where
(i_{x},j_{y})
is the cell in
D
corresponding to the cell
(d+1-x,y)
in
[\widehat{\mu}]
, then
i_{x}=d+1-x-c
, and
j_{y}=y+c
for some nonnegative integer
c
(how many times the cell
(d+1-x,y)
was excited to obtain
(i_{x},j_{y})
). Since
\lambda/\mu
is slim, we have that
\lambda^{\prime}_{j_{x}}=d
. Thus the hooklength of cell
(i_{x},j_{y})
equals

h(i_{x},j_{y})=\lambda_{i_{x}}-i_{x}+\lambda^{\prime}_{j_{y}}-j_{y}+1=\lambda_{d+1-T(x,y)}+x-y,

as desired.
∎

Example 5.10
.

Figure
12
illustrates an excited diagram
D
in
\mathcal{E}^{\nearrow}(\lambda/\mu)
for the slim shape
\lambda/\mu=775/21
, its corresponding tableau
T=\Psi(D)
in
\mathcal{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
, and the corresponding reverse excited diagram
D^{\prime}
from
T
. The weight of the hook-lengths of
D
is
8\cdot 6\cdot 5
which matches the weight of the broken diagonals of
D^{\prime}
.

Corollary 5.11
.

For a slim skew shape
\lambda/\mu
of size
n
we have that the variation of the NHLF in (
5.1
) is term-by-term equal to (
OOF
).

Proof.

This follows immediately by applying the bijection
\Psi
, which is weight preserving by Proposition
5.9
, to rewrite (
5.1
) in terms of Okounkov–Olshanski tableaux to obtain (
OOF
).
∎

Figure 12
.
Example of the weight preserving bijection between an excited diagrams in
\mathcal{E}^{\nearrow}(\lambda/\mu)
(left) and a reverse excited diagrams in
\mathcal{RED}(\lambda/\mu)
(right) for a slim shape via tableaux in
\mathcal{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
(middle).

6.
Hillman-Grassl bijection on minimal skew tableaux

In this section we show that the Hillman–Grassl bijection restricted to the minimal SSYT of shape
\lambda/\mu
is equivalent to the inverse of bijection
\Phi
from Section
3
. Recall the definition of the array
A_{D}
for an excited diagram
D
in
\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)
and of the arrays
\mathcal{A}^{*}(\lambda/\mu)
from Section
2.6
.

6.1.
Equivalence of Hillman–Grassl map and
\Phi^{-1}
on minimal SSYT

We state the main result of this section.

\PhivsHG

To prove this result we will need the following two lemmas that use the known connection between the Hillman–Grassl correspondence and the RSK bijection (see Theorem
2.26
). Let
A({\gamma_{i}(D)})
be the a subarray of
A_{D}
of shape
\lambda\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{i-1}\gamma_{j}(D)
with support on the broken diagonal of
\gamma_{i}(D)
.

Lemma 6.1
.

For
D\in\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)
and
k
an integer
1-\ell(\lambda)\leq k\leq\lambda_{1}-1
, let
T:=HG^{-1}(A_{D})
and
\nu=\nu^{(k)}
be the partition whose parts are the entries in the
k
-diagonal of
T
. Then
\nu_{i}=|A({\gamma_{i}(D)})_{k}|
, that is
\nu_{i}
is the number of broken diagonals in
\gamma_{i}(D)\cap\square^{\lambda}_{k}
.

Proof.

Since
A_{D}=HG(T)
, by Theorem
2.26
it suffices to show that
ac_{t}(A_{k})=a_{1}+a_{2}+\cdots+a_{t}
, where
a_{i}=|A({\gamma_{i}(D)})_{k}|
. We consider only
\gamma_{i}(D)
’s which intersect
\square^{\lambda}_{k}
and index them in order from the NW-most to the SE-most within
\square^{\lambda}_{k}
(we can order them, because every such path will intersect the diagonal
j-i=k
).

First, we note that
a_{1}>a_{2}>\cdots
: this is because
a_{i}
is equal to the height of
\gamma_{i}(D)
minus 1, and the paths within
\square^{\lambda}_{k}
start along the lower horizontal border at points
P_{i}
and end on the right vertical border at points
Q_{i}
, so they end below each other.

Next, note that
ac_{t}(A_{k})\geq a_{1}+\cdots+a_{t}
, because the 1s in each
\gamma_{i}(D)
form a NE path (i.e. increasing sequence).

To show the opposite inequality, we consider the broken diagonals in
\square^{\lambda}_{k}
, that is
\mathrm{Br}(D)\cap\square^{\lambda}_{k}
. Let
\alpha_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\alpha_{t}
be a disjoint collection of maximal nonoverlapping NE paths, so
ac_{t}(A_{k})=|\alpha_{1}|+\cdots+|\alpha_{t}|
. We can assume that these paths are nonintersecting also, as we can relabel their parts at intersections. The diagonal steps in each broken diagonal form a SE monotone path, i.e. no squares in the same row or column. Thus for every broken diagonal
d_{i}
, we must have
|d_{i}\cap\alpha_{j}|\leq 1
. Thus,

ac_{t}(A_{k})\leq\sum_{i}\max\{|d_{i}\cap\square^{\lambda}_{k}|,t\}.

We now show that this upper bound is exactly the contribution from the
\gamma(D)
’s. We have that
d_{i}\cap\square^{\lambda}_{k}
consists of the vertical steps of the
\gamma(D)
paths which intersect that broken diagonal. As the
\gamma
paths are greedily selected from the inner shape outwards, the first paths will contain the first (NW-most) points from the corresponding
d_{i}
’s. Thus

|\{d_{i}\cap\square^{\lambda}_{k}\}|=\max\{j:\gamma_{j}(D)\cap d_{i}\cap\square^{\lambda}_{k}\},

and since we are looking at the maximum
t
many, we naturally limit ourselves to
j\leq t
. Thus
we have

ac_{t}(A_{k})\leq\sum_{i}\max\{|d_{i}\cap\square^{\lambda}_{k}|,t\}=|\gamma_{1}(D)\cap\square^{\lambda}_{k}|+\cdots+|\gamma_{t}(D)\cap\square^{\lambda}_{k}|=a_{1}+\cdots+a_{t},

and the proof is done.

∎

The next lemma confirms that counting broken diagonals in the rectangular subarray
(A_{D})_{k}
is another way of describing the bijection
\Phi
.

Lemma 6.2
.

For
D\in\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)
and
k
an integer
1-\ell(\lambda)\leq k\leq\lambda_{1}-1
, let
T^{\prime}:=\Phi(D)\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)
and and
\nu=\nu^{(k)}
be the partition whose parts are the entries in the
k
-diagonal of
T^{\prime}
. Then
\nu_{i}=|A({\gamma_{i}(D)})_{k}|
, that is
\nu_{i}
is the number of broken diagonals in
\gamma_{i}(D)\cap\square^{\lambda}_{k}
.

Proof.

First, recall that
T^{\prime}
is decomposed into Lascoux–Pragacz paths
\theta_{i},\dots,\theta_{m}
, where
\theta_{1}
is the of
\lambda/\mu
wrapping around
\lambda
. Then for the
k
-diagonal of
T^{\prime}
with values
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{t}
, the cell with the value
\nu_{i}
is contained in
\theta_{i^{\prime}}
for some
i^{\prime}\geq i
, where
\epsilon_{i^{\prime}}=i-1
and the cell
(u_{1},v_{1})\in\theta_{1}
with value
\nu_{1}
is always the most outer element of the
k
-diagonal. Note that the cell
(u_{1},v_{1})
is also the most outer corner of the corresponding
\square^{\lambda}_{k}
in
A_{D}
. Also, as stated in Definition
3.3
, the
j
th column of
\gamma_{i^{\prime}}(D)
corresponds to the
j
th column of
\theta_{i^{\prime}}
, starting from the left-most column. Denote
t_{i}
and
s_{i}
the starting column and row of
\theta_{i^{\prime}}
respectively. Let
(u,v)
be the cell with value
\nu_{i}
, which is on the
j
th column of
\theta_{i^{\prime}}
, i.e. column
t_{i}+j-1
. Then by the construction of
\Phi
in Definition
3.3
(1) and (2), we know that
\nu_{i}=T^{\prime}(u,v)
is the number of accumulated broken diagonals of
\gamma_{i}(D)
until the
j
th column of this path and by Definition
3.3
(3) we need to subtract the number
s_{i}-u
of north steps from the initial cell, thus

(6.1)

\nu_{i}=|A(\gamma_{i}(D))|_{j}-s_{i}+u,

where
|A(\gamma_{i}^{\prime}(D))|_{j}
is the number of broken diagonals of
\gamma_{i^{\prime}}(D)
until the
jth
column.
For the Kreiman paths in
A_{D}
, denote by
g_{i}
and
f_{i}
the starting column and row of
\gamma_{i^{\prime}}(D)
, respectively. The
j
th column of
\gamma_{i^{\prime}}(D)
is then
g_{i}+j-1
. By Lemma
2.8
we know that
g_{i}-t_{i}=\epsilon_{i^{\prime}}
, the diagonal distance from
\theta_{1}
to
\theta_{i}
(and similarly, also
f_{i}-s_{i}=\epsilon_{i^{\prime}}
). So the
j
th column of
\gamma_{i}(D)
is
t_{i}+j-1+\epsilon_{i}
. Recall that the cell with value
\nu_{i}
of the
k
-diagonal is on column
t_{i}+j-1
. Then the column
t_{i}+j-1+\epsilon_{i^{\prime}}
, the
j
th column of
\gamma_{i}(D)
, is the last column in
\square^{\lambda}_{k}
. Also the rectangle
\square_{k}^{\lambda}
does not include the broken diagonals of
\gamma_{i}(D)
that lie below the position
(u_{1},v_{1})
, so we need to subtract
f_{i}-u_{1}
many broken diagonals from
|A(\gamma_{i}(D))|
to obtain
|A(\gamma_{i}(D))_{k}|
. That is, we have that

(6.2)

|A(\gamma_{i}(D))_{k}|=|A(\gamma_{i}(D))|_{j}-f_{i}-u_{1}.

Note that the diagonal distance between the cell with the value
\nu_{i}
and
\nu_{1}
is
\epsilon_{i}
. Then
u_{1}-u=\epsilon_{i}
. Then by Lemma
2.8
,
f_{i}-u_{1}=s_{i}+\epsilon_{i}-(u-\epsilon_{i})=s_{i}-u
. Combining this with (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) we obtain that
\nu_{i}=|A({\gamma_{i}(D)})_{k}|
, as desired.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

For an excited diagram
D\in\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)
, let
T:=HG^{-1}(A_{D})
and
T^{\prime}=\Phi(D)
. By Lemma
6.1
and Lemma
6.2
, the tableaux
T
and
T^{\prime}
agree on each
k
-diagonal for
1-\ell(\lambda)\leq k\leq\lambda_{1}-1
. Thus
T=T^{\prime}
as desired.
∎

Example 6.3
.

Figure
13(a)
shows an excited diagram
D
for the shape
\lambda/\mu=55552/21
with its broken diagonals and Kreiman paths
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
and the tableau
T=HG^{-1}(A_{D})=\Phi(D)
. The parts of the partition
\nu=\nu^{(1)}=321
obtained from the
1
-diagonal of
T
can be read from the number of broken diagonals in
\gamma_{i}(D)
inside the rectangle
\square_{1}^{\lambda}
for
i=1,2,3
, respectively.

Remark 6.4
.

Lemma
6.1
does not hold for general excited arrays with nonnegative entries other than
0/1
. This is because the longest ascending chain might involve entries from distinct
\gamma_{i}
. See Example
6.5
.

Example 6.5
.

Figure
13(b)
shows an excited array
A^{\prime}_{D}
for the same excited diagram as Example
6.3
and the tableau
T^{\prime}=HG^{-1}(A^{\prime}_{D})
. The first part
5
of the partition
\nu=\nu^{(1)}=521
obtained from the
1
-diagonal of
T^{\prime}
corresponds to an ascending chain involving entries from two paths
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 13
.
(a) Illustration of the relation between Greene’s theorem and the map
\Phi
. The parts of
\nu
encode the number of broken diagonals in each
\gamma_{i}(D)
inside the rectangle
\square^{\lambda}_{k}
. (b) Counterexample of why this relation does not hold if the entries of the array are other than

.

6.2.
Leading terms of the SSYT
q
-analogue of Naruse’s formula

In this section we give an expression for the leading terms of (
1.5
).

\leadtermsqNHLF

Proof.

From Theorem
1.2
and the fact that the Hillman–Grassl bijection is weight preserving (
2.3
) we have that

\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)}q^{|T|}\,=\,\sum_{D\in\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)}q^{\sum_{u\in A_{D}}h(u)}.

By
[
16
, Prop. 7.16]
we have that
\sum_{u\in A_{D}}h(u)=\sum_{(i,j)\in\lambda\setminus D}(\lambda^{\prime}_{j}-i)
and the result follows.
∎

This result motivates calling the polynomial in (
1.6
) a
q
-analogue of the number of excited diagrams of shape
\lambda/\mu
and denoting it by
\mathcal{E}_{q}(\lambda/\mu)
. Next, we give some examples of this polynomial. It would be of interest to study
\mathcal{E}_{q}(\lambda/\mu)
further.

Example 6.6
.

For the shape
\lambda/\mu=55332/22
whose excited diagrams and minimal tableaux are illustrated in Figures
7
,
6
we have that

\mathcal{E}_{q}(\lambda/\mu)\,=\,q^{14}(q^{2}+q^{3}+q^{4}+q^{5}+q^{6}).

Example 6.7
.

Let
m
and
n
be nonnegative integers. For the reverse hook
\lambda/\mu=(m+1)^{n+1}/m^{n}
, the number of excited diagrams is given by
\binom{n+m}{n}
(Proposition
4.4
) and these diagrams are in bijection with lattice paths
\gamma
from
(m+1,1)
to
(1,n+1)
inside the
(m+1)\times(n+1)
rectangle (see
[
16
, Ex. 3.2]
). A direct consequence of
[
16
, Ex. 3.2]
and
[
29
, Prop. 1.7.3]
is the following

\mathcal{E}_{q}((m+1)^{n+1}/m^{n})\,=\,q^{\binom{n+1}{2}}\sum_{\gamma:(m+1,1)\to(1,n+1)}q^{\textup{area}(\gamma)}\,=\,q^{\binom{n+1}{2}}{\begin{bmatrix}n+m\\
n\end{bmatrix}_{q}},

where
\textup{area}(\gamma)
is the number of cells in the rectangle
(m+1)\times(n+1)
South East of
\gamma
, and
{\begin{bmatrix}a\\
b\end{bmatrix}_{q}}
denotes the
q
-binomial coefficient.

Example 6.8
.

For the reverse hook
\lambda/\mu=\delta_{n+2}/\delta_{n}
, the number of excited diagrams is given by the Catalan number
C_{n}
(Proposition
4.3
) and these diagrams are in bijection with Dyck paths (see
[
15
, §8]
). Let
\textup{Dyck}(n)
be the set of Dyck paths
\mathsf{p}
of size
n
, a direct consequence of
[
15
, Cor. 1.7]
is the following

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{q}(\delta_{n+2}/\delta_{n})\,=\,\sum_{\mathsf{p}\in\textup{Dyck}(n)}q^{\sum_{(a,b)\in\mathsf{p}}b}\,=\,q^{n}\sum_{\mathsf{p}\in\textup{Dyck}(n)}q^{2\cdot\textup{area}(\mathsf{p})}\,=\,q^{n}C_{n}(q^{2}),

where
\textup{area}(\mathsf{p})
denotes the area of the Dyck path
\mathsf{p}
and
C_{n}(q)
denotes the
q
-Catalan number (see
[
5
, Ch. 1]
).

6.3.
Additivity consequences

In this section as a consequence of Theorem
1.2
, we derive some linearity properties of the Hillman–Grassl correspondence on minimal SSYT.

Given
T
in
\mathrm{SSYT}(\lambda/\mu)
, let
T_{\theta_{i}}
be the tableau of shape
\lambda\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{i-1}\theta_{j}
with entries of
T
in
\theta_{i}
. We define the sum
A_{\gamma_{i}(D)}+A_{\gamma_{i+1}(D)}
as an array of the same shape as
A_{\gamma_{i}(D)}
with supports on the broken diagonals of
\gamma_{i}(D)
and
\gamma_{i+1}(D)
. In other words the array value of
(u,v)\in A_{\gamma_{i}}+A_{\gamma_{i+1}}
is
A_{\gamma_{i}}(u,v)+A_{\gamma_{i+1}}(u-t,v-t)
where
t=\epsilon_{i+1}-\epsilon_{i}
.

Corollary 6.9
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)
and
T=\Phi(D)\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)
. Then for
i=1,\ldots,k
, we have that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}HG(T_{\theta_{i}})=HG(T).

Proof.

As mentioned above, we have that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}A_{\gamma_{i}(D)}=A_{D}
and that the tableau
T
decomposes as a disjoint union of
\bigcup_{i}T_{\theta_{i}}
of tableaux
T_{\theta_{i}}
in each border strip. By Theorem
1.2
we have that
HG(T)=A_{D}
. By construction we have that
T=\bigcup_{i}T_{\theta_{i}}
. Since by Definition
3.3
, the bijection
\Phi
builds
T_{\theta_{i}}
just from information in
\gamma_{i}(D)
then
\Phi(A_{\gamma_{i}(D)})=T_{\theta_{i}}
. By Theorem
1.2
applied to the border strip
\theta_{i}
we then have that
HG(T_{\theta_{i}})=A_{\gamma_{i}(D)}
and the result follows.
∎

Example 6.10
.

Consider the skew shape
\lambda/\mu=55552/21
with Lascoux–Pragacz decomposition
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}
and the excited diagram
D
in
\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)
with Kreiman decomposition
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
at the bottom of Figure
15
. For
T=\Phi(D)
we have that
HG(T_{\theta_{1}})+HG(T_{\theta_{2}})+HG(T_{\theta_{3}})=HG(T)
as illustrated in the figure.

We can also view Corollary
6.9
as a statement about a rearrangement of the lattice paths involved in the inverse Hillman–Grassl map for each broken diagonal in
A_{\gamma_{i}(D)}
and each broken diagonal in
A_{D}
. To state this result, we introduce some notation. For each broken diagonal support
b\in A_{\gamma_{i}(D)}
(
b\in A_{D}
respectively), denote the collection of cells in the lattice path built for its inverse Hillman-Grassl step by
L(b;\theta_{i})
(
L(b)
respectively).

Corollary 6.11
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)
be an excited diagram with Kreiman decomposition
(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k})
, then as multisets we have that

(6.3)

\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\Biggl(\bigcup_{b\in\mathrm{Br}(\gamma_{i})}L(b;\theta_{i})\Biggr)=\bigcup_{b\in\mathrm{Br}(D)}L(b).

Figure 14
.
Convention for adding arrays
A_{\gamma_{1}(D)}
and
A_{\gamma_{2}(D)}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.2
we have that
HG^{-1}(A_{D})=T
and as explained in the proof of Corollary
6.9
, from Theorem
1.2
and Definition
3.3
we also have that
HG^{-1}(A_{\gamma_{i}(D)})=T_{\theta_{i}}
. Then for each
(r,s)\in\theta_{i}
, the entry
T(r,s)
is the multiplicity of
(r,s)
in the multiset in the RHS of (
6.3
) and this entry
T(r,s)=T_{\theta_{i}}(r,s)
is also the multiplicity of
(r,s)
in the multiset
\bigcup_{b\in\mathrm{Br}(\gamma_{i})}L(b;\theta_{i})
in the LHS of (
6.3
). The equality of this multisets follows.
∎

Example 6.12
.

See Figure
19
for an example of the equality of multisets in (
6.3
). For instance, the entries in the paths in the first column highlighted in red equal the entries in the second and third column highlighted in blue.

Figure 15
.
The Hillman-Grassl map of
T
, and each of
T_{\theta_{1}}
,
T_{\theta_{2}}
, and
T_{\theta_{3}}
separately

7.
Bijective proof of Theorem
2.28
for SSYT of border strips

In the previous section we showed that
\Phi^{-1}
corresponds to the Hillman–Grassl map restricted to minimal SSYT of shape
\lambda/\mu
, giving a bijective proof of a special case of Theorem
2.28
. In this section we prove bijectively another case of this theorem for SSYT whose shapes are border strips.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\lambda/\mu
be a border strip and
T
be a SSYT of shape
\lambda/\mu
. Then
HG(T)\in\mathcal{A}^{*}_{D}
for some excited diagram
D
in
\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)
.

The first step is to show that the support of the resulting array
A=HG(T)
is in
\mathcal{A}_{D}
for some excited diagram
D
in
\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)
corresponding to some lattice path
\gamma
from
(\lambda^{\prime}_{1},1)
to
(1,\lambda_{1})
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\lambda/\mu
be a border strip and
T
be a SSYT of shape
\lambda/\mu
. Then
HG(T)\in\mathcal{A}^{*}_{D}
for some excited diagram
D
in
\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)
, and in particular the nonzero entries of the array are contained in a SW to NE lattice path.

Proof.

Since the shape of
T
is a border strip, for an integer
k
with
1-\ell(\lambda)\leq k\leq\lambda_{1}-1
, by Theorem
2.26
(ii) we have that
dc_{1}(A_{k})=1
. Then the nonzero entries in each rectangle
A_{k}
form a NE lattice path for each
k
. Naturally, if two rectangles
A_{k}
corresponding to different
k
’s overlap, then paths in each should also coincide at the intersection of the two rectangles, and so for each
k
we get portions of one large NE lattice path.
∎

We define a piece-wise linear map
C
from border strip SSYT of shape
\lambda/\mu
to lattice paths in
[\lambda]
with nonnegative integer entries as follows.
Let
T
be a border strip SSYT of shape
\lambda/\mu
, such that
T=T^{\prime}\cup T_{1}\cup T_{2}
, where
T_{2}=[a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}]^{\top}
is the last column of
T
, indexed
j
, and
T_{1}=[b_{k}]
is attached to
T_{2}
horizontally on the left. Let
r=\max\{i\,\mid\,a_{k}-b_{k}>a_{i-1}\}
where we assume
a_{0}=0
. Set
p_{i}:=a_{i}-a_{i-1}
for
i=1,\ldots,r-1
, where
a_{0}=0
and set
p_{r}:=a_{k}-b_{k}-a_{r-1}
.

Let
R:=T^{\prime}\cup[b_{r},\ldots,b_{k}]^{\top}
, i.e the border strip SSYT comprised of
T^{\prime}
and the column attached on its right, where

b_{i}:=a_{i}-(a_{k}-b_{k})
for
i=r,\ldots,k
.

Then define
C(T):=C(R)\cup[p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}]^{\top}
recursively as a path in
[\lambda]
ending at
(1,j)
, where
C(R)
is a path in
[\lambda]
ending at
(r,j-1)
and the entries at
(i,j)
for
i\geq r
of
C(T)
are equal to
p_{i}
. See Figure
16
for an example of
C(T)
.

Figure 16
.
Illustration of the map
C(T)
from border strip SSYT to lattice paths in
[\lambda/\mu]
with nonnegative integer entries that coincides with
HG(T)
. The cells of
T^{\prime}
are filled in green, the cell of
T_{1}
is filled in gray, and the cell
a_{r}
has a red border.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
\lambda/\mu
be a border strip and
T
be a SSYT of shape
\lambda/\mu
. Then
HG(T)
has support in a SW to NE lattice path, such that all up steps are strictly positive entries and the entries are piece–wise linear functions of the entries of
T
. Moreover,
HG(T)=C(T)
for
C(\cdot)
as defined above.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.2
the nonzero entries of
HG(T)=A
form a SW to NE lattice path, so we need to show the shape and entries match
C(T)
.
Let
T
be a border strip SSYT as above. Let also entry
a_{k}
be in the
t
-th diagonal of the shape. By Theorem
2.26
we have that
|A_{t}|=a_{k}
and
|A_{t-1}|=b_{k}
and
|A_{t+i}|=a_{k-i}
. Moreover, since each diagonal of
T
is a partition of one part, say
\nu=(\nu_{1})
at diagonal
p
, then
|A_{p}|=ac_{1}(A_{p})=\nu_{1}
. Thus, since
HG
is a bijection, to show that
HG(T)=C(T)
, which both have supports on lattice paths, it is enough to show that the sum of entries in
A_{p}
and
C(T)_{p}
coincide for all diagonals
p
.
This is now straightforward to check by the explicit form of
C(T)
and induction on the number of columns.

Finally, we note that in the last column of
C(T)
, the up steps of the lattice path have entries
p_{i}>0
because
a_{i}>a_{i-1}
. Also,
R
is a border strip SSYT since
b_{i}-b_{i-1}=a_{i}-a_{i-1}>0
with nonzero entries up to row
r+1
, and by induction
C(R)
is a lattice path ending in
(r,j)
with nonzero entries on the up steps.

Thus the sum of entries in the last column of
A_{t}
is equal to
a_{k}-b
. Similarly, the sum of entries in
A_{t+i}
is
a_{k-i}
. Since the support of
A
is a SW to NE lattice path, let the support in the last column extend to row
r
and then the path turns horizontally, i.e. going west. Let the entries along the path be
p_{1},\ldots,p_{r},q_{1},\ldots
so that the entries in the last column are
p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}
with
p_{r}>0
. Then for
i<r
we have

p_{1}+\cdots+p_{i}=|A_{t+k-i}|=a_{i}
, so
p_{i}=a_{i}-a_{i-1}>0
. Next, the path takes a turn at
i=r
, so we have
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{r}=|A_{t}|-|A_{t-1}|=a_{k}-b
and
p_{r}=a_{k}-b-a_{r-1}>0
by the choice of
r
. The remaining path is
HG(T^{\prime})
, where
T^{\prime}
is a border strip SSYT which coincides with
T
until the last two columns. Note that by the choice of
r
we have that
b=|A_{t-1}|\geq a_{k}-a_{r}
since the nonzero entries in
A_{t}\setminus A_{r}
are in
A_{t-1}
. Thus we have that
b\geq a_{k}-a_{r}>a_{k-1}-p_{r}>\ldots>a_{r}-p_{r}
which is again a border strip SSYT and by induction we recover the other entries of
A
.
∎

8.
Additivity of Hillman–Grassl for straight shapes

In this section we show the following result.

\additivityHGstraight

Before we proceed with the proof of this fact we will show a useful property of the ascending chains.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
A
be a rectangular array on
[a]\times[b]
with nonnegative entries and
ac_{t}(A)
be the maximal weight in the disjoint union of
t
ascending chains in
A
. Then there exists a collection of disjoint connected border-strip paths of boxes
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{t}
in
A
, such that
\rho_{i}
starts at
(1,i)
and ends at
(a,b-i+1)
, and

\sum_{i}\sum_{p\in\rho_{i}}A_{p}=ac_{t}(A),

i.e. the total weight of entries in this collection is maximal.

This is saying that the maximal ascending chains can be made to start and end at the sides of the rectangle.

Proof.

First, we observe that if there are two disjoint ascending chains they can be made non-crossing (i.e. one is above the other). To see this, form monotone piecewise linear paths
\tau^{1},\tau^{2}
from the centers of the squares of these chains, with starting and ending points
P_{1},Q_{1}
and
P_{2},Q_{2}
, respectively. Suppose that these paths cross at points
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{k}
. We can then assign the piecewise linear paths
\tau^{\prime}:=\tau^{1}(P_{1}\to X_{1})\tau^{2}(X_{1}\to X_{2})\tau^{1}(X_{2}\to X_{3})\cdots
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}:=\tau^{2}(P_{2}\to X_{1})\tau^{1}(X_{1}\to X_{2})\cdots
. These paths cover the same set of squares, and are monotonous (i.e. ascending), but now do not cross over.

We can now assume that the paths do not cross. We now proceed with a greedy algorithm to relabel the paths to satisfy the condition in the statement. Let
D
be the union of squares giving a maximal
ac_{t}(A)
. Since the weights in
A
are nonnegative, adding boxes to the paths in
D
will only increase the total weight. Thus, we can assume that
D
extends to the borders of
A
. Let
\rho_{1}
be the bottom-most squares of
D
. If it does not reach the lowest row in
A
, we can add/reassign those boxes to
\rho_{1}
, so it contains
(1,1)
. Suppose that
(1,1),(1,2),\ldots,(1,i)\in\rho_{1}
for some
i
. As the other paths are above
\rho_{1}
, we can extend one, say
\rho_{2}
to contain
(1,i+1)
. We then assign boxes
(1,2),\ldots,(1,i)
from
\rho_{1}
to
\rho_{2}
, keeping it ascending and preserving the weight, and add
(1,1),(2,1),(2,2),\ldots,(2,i)
to
\rho_{1}
. If
t>2
, we can then assign boxes
(1,3),(1,4),\ldots
to
\rho_{3}
, assign
(1,2),(2,2),(2,3),\ldots
from
\rho_{1}
to
\rho_{2}
and add
(3,1),(3,2),\ldots
to
\rho_{1}
.
Similarly, we can perform the same procedure on the other side of
A
by relabeling paths and possibly assigning extra boxes to them. This procedure, greedily pulling the paths SE from the border of
D
, by possibly adding boxes, will make and keep the paths connected.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Let
A=HG(T)
,
M=HG(T_{min})
. The statement will follow directly from the RSK connection with HG (Theorem
2.26
) if we show that for every
k
and
t
we have

ac_{t}(A_{k}+M_{k})=ac_{t}(A_{k})+ac_{t}(M_{k}).

We have that
M_{k}
consists of
1
below the main diagonal, and 0s above. For simplicity, we will identify
M_{k}
as the collection of boxes with values 1, i.e. its support.

We now apply Proposition
8.1
to
A_{k}+M_{k}
and
A_{k}
. Assume that the rectangles are
[a]\times[b]
(so
b-a=k
) and that
a\leq b
, otherwise we can reflect the application of the Proposition, so that the paths’ endpoints be
(i,1)
and
(a-i+1,b)
. Let
D
be the collection of
t
ascending chains of maximal weight for
A_{k}
, according to Proposition
8.1
we can choose them to start at
(1,i)
and end at
(a,b-i+1)
and be continuous border strips. Let
D^{\prime}
be the corresponding collection of
t
ascending chains of maximal weight for
A_{k}+M_{k}
with the same properties. If
\rho_{i}^{\prime}
is a continuous monotonous path from
(1,i)
to
(a,b-i+1)
then
|\rho_{i}^{\prime}\cap M_{k}|=b-i
(the grid distance between
(1,i)
and the main diagonal). Thus

\rho^{\prime}_{i}(A_{k}+M_{k})=\rho^{\prime}_{i}(A_{k})+b-i
and so

ac_{t}(A_{k}+M_{k})=D^{\prime}(A_{k})+(b-1)+\cdots+(b-t),

where
D^{\prime}(A_{k})
is the total weight of the boxes
D^{\prime}
on
A_{k}
. Thus, the above weight is maximal iff the total weight
D^{\prime}(A_{k})
is maximal. Since
D^{\prime}
is a collection of
t
disjoint ascending chains, then

\max_{D^{\prime}}D^{\prime}(A_{k})=ac_{t}(A_{k}).

Since
ac_{t}(M_{k})=(b-1)+\cdots+(b-t)
, we thus get that

ac_{t}(A_{k}+M_{k})=ac_{t}(A_{k})+ac_{t}(M_{k})

for every
t
and
k
and the claim follows.
∎

The following example shows that Theorem
1.2
does not hold for skew shapes.

Figure 17
.
Counterexample for additivity of Hillman–Grassl for skew shapes.

Example 8.2
.

Let
T
be the skew SSYT of shape
\lambda/\mu=55552/21
in Figure
17
and
T_{\min}:=\Phi(D)
be the minimal SSYT corresponding to the excited diagram
D=\{(1,1),(3,2),(3,4)\}
. As illustrated in the figure,
HG(T)+HG(T_{\min})\neq HG(T+T_{\min})
.

Figure 18
.
Counterexample for additivity of Hillman–Grassl for skew shapes.

For straight shapes, given a SSYT
T
of shape
\lambda
and
T_{\min}=T_{\min}(\lambda)
, then
T-T_{\min}(\lambda)
is a reverse plane partition. This does no hold for skew shapes as the next example shows.

Example 8.3
.

Let
T
be the skew RPP of shape
\lambda/\mu=22222/11
in Figure
18
that corresponds to the excited diagram
D=\{(1,1),(3,1)\}
. If
T_{\min}=\Phi(D)
then
T-T_{\min}
is not a reverse plane partition.

9.
Final Remarks

9.1.
A bijective proof of Theorem
1.6

This article was in part motivated towards giving an entirely combinatorial proof of Theorem
1.6
from
[
16
]
. From Theorem
1.2
and Corollary
1.2
, we understand combinatorially the leading terms of each summand on the right-hand-side of (
1.5
) in terms of the minimal SSYT of shape
\lambda/\mu
. Also, in Section
7
we have a bijective proof for border strips
\lambda/\mu
. It would be interesting to understand combinatorially other cases like shapes with Lascoux–Pragacz decomposition
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
or
thick zig-zags

\delta_{n+k}/\delta_{n}
.

9.2.
Comparison with the formula for skew tableaux involving Littlewood–Richardson coefficients

In Section
5
we compared the number of terms of the positive formulas (
NHLF
) and (
OOF
) for
f^{\lambda/\mu}
and showed that
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)\leq\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
.
Another positive formula for
f^{\lambda/\mu}
is given using the
Littlewood-Richardson coefficients

c^{\lambda}_{\mu,\nu}
, namely

(9.1)

f^{\lambda/\mu}\,=\,\sum_{\nu\vdash|\lambda/\mu|}c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}f^{\nu}.

The terms
f^{\nu}
are readily computed by the classical hook-length formula (
1.1
). It is natural to compare this formula to (
NHLF
) and (
OOF
) and see which one is more “efficient”, in the sense of number of summands. Since the
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
do not have explicit product formulas we will regard them as multiplicities and thus the question is to compare

\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu),\,\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)\qquad\text{vs}\qquad\mathrm{LR}(\lambda/\mu):=\sum_{\nu\vdash n}c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}.

From one of the rules to compute
c^{\lambda}_{\mu,\nu}
(see
[
28
, Ch. 7, A1.3]
),
\mathrm{LR}(\lambda/\mu)
counts the number of
Littlewood–Richardson tableaux
: which are SSYT
T
of skew shape
\lambda/\mu
with positive entries whose
reverse reading word
(reading the entries of
T
row by row right-to-left and bottom-to-top) is a
lattice permutation
, i.e. in every initial factor of the word the number of
i
s is at least as many as the number of
(i+1)
s, for each
i
. Denote the set of such tableaux by
\mathcal{LR}(\lambda/\mu)
. From this interpretation of
\mathrm{LR}(\lambda/\mu)=|\mathcal{LR}(\lambda/\mu)|
we obtain the following inequality.

Proposition 9.1
.

For a skew shape
\lambda/\mu
, we have that
\mathrm{LR}(\lambda/\mu)\leq\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
.

Proof.

We show that
\mathcal{LR}(\lambda/\mu)\subseteq\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)
which implies the result since by Proposition
2.19
, we have that
|\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)|=\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
.

Given a tableau
T
in
\mathcal{LR}(\lambda/\mu)
, by the lattice condition of its reverse reading word
w
, the entries in the first row of
T
are all
1
s. Iteratively, if the entries of
T
in row
i
are at most
i
, then the word
w
up until that point has entries at most
i
. Then the last entry of row
i+1
, which comes next in
w
is at most
i
by the lattice condition. Thus the entries of
T
in row
i+1
are at most
i
and so
T\in\mathcal{SF}(\lambda/\mu)
, as desired.
∎

Example 9.2
.

A quick computation for
\lambda/\mu=4321/21
shows that
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)=5
,
\mathrm{LR}(\lambda/\mu)=11
, and
\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)=17
. On the other hand when
\lambda/\mu=43/2
we have
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)=\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)=3
and
\mathrm{LR}(\lambda/\mu)=2
. Also when
\lambda/\mu=8642/21
we have
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)=8
and
\mathrm{LR}(\lambda/\mu)=\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)=20
.

From these examples, the question arises for which shapes
\lambda/\mu
are:

\mathrm{LR}\leq\mathrm{ED}\leq\mathrm{OOT},\quad\text{ or }\quad\mathrm{ED}\leq\mathrm{LR}\leq\mathrm{OOT}?

In the first example above we see that the minimal SSYT tableaux are actually Littlewood–Richardson tableaux. This also raises the question of which shapes
\lambda/\mu
give equalities
\mathrm{ED}(\lambda/\mu)=\mathrm{LR}(\lambda/\mu)
and
\mathrm{LR}(\lambda/\mu)=\mathrm{OOT}(\lambda/\mu)
, respectively?

9.3.
Multivariate identity from the Hillman–Grassl bijection

In
[
21
, Thm. 1.2]
, Naurse–Okada gave a multivariate identity for skew RPP. Because the Hillman–Grassl bijection is behind the
q
-analogue (
1.5
), we can derive an analogous multivariate identity of skew SSYT. We need some notation. The variables are
\mathbf{z}=(z_{i})_{i\in I}
, where
I\subset\mathbb{Z}
is the set of contents of
\lambda
, that is the set of integers
\{1-\ell(\lambda),\dots,\lambda_{1}-1\}
. For
\pi\in\mathrm{RPP}(\lambda/\mu)
, we let

\mathbf{z}^{\pi}:=\prod_{u\in[\lambda/\mu]}z_{c(u)}^{\pi(u)},\qquad\mathbf{z}[H_{\lambda}(u)]:=\prod_{v\in H_{\lambda}(u)}z_{c(v)},

where
H_{\lambda}(u)
are the cells of
[\lambda/\mu]
in the hook of cell
u\in[\lambda/\mu]
. Combining Theorem
2.28
and Proposition
2.25
gives the following identity.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
\lambda/\mu
be a skew shape. We have the following multivariate identity:

\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\lambda/\mu)}\mathbf{z}^{T}\,=\,\sum_{D\in\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)}\frac{\prod_{u\in\mathrm{Br}(D)}\mathbf{z}[H_{\lambda}(u)]}{\prod_{u\in D}(1-\mathbf{z}[H_{\lambda}(u)])}.

We end with a multivariate analogue of Corollary
1.2
obtained by combining Theorem
1.2
and Proposition
2.25
.

Corollary 9.4
.

Let
\lambda/\mu
be a skew shape. We have the following multivariate identity:

\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\min}(\lambda/\mu)}\mathbf{z}^{T}\,=\,\sum_{D\in\mathcal{E}(\lambda/\mu)}\prod_{u\in\mathrm{Br}(D)}\mathbf{z}[H_{\lambda}(u)].

]
,

.

The first two columns show the inverse
Hillman-Grassl paths
L(b)
on
A_{D}
for a diagram
D
with Kreiman decomposition
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
. The next columns show the paths
L(b;\theta_{1})
on
A_{\gamma_{1}}
and the paths
L(b;\theta_{2})
on
A_{\gamma_{2}}
respectively. By Corollary
6.11
, the multiset
\bigcup_{b\in\mathrm{Br}(D)}L(b)
equals the union of the multisets
\bigcup_{b\in\mathrm{Br}(\gamma_{i})}L(b;\theta_{i})
for
i=1,2
.
